People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… yeah, I guess what finally scared me enough to come in is I’ve been hearing my name called when nobody’s there. Like at home, from the hallway, and I’ll think it’s my husband, because it sounds that real, like he’s standing right next to me. But then I go look and there’s nobody.

It happened once at the warehouse too, which really threw me off, because I was stocking and it’s noisy there sometimes, forklifts and pallets and people calling out, so at first I thought, okay, maybe I just misheard. But… nobody was there. And I don’t know, I kept thinking about it all night after, and then I was like, maybe I should actually talk to somebody before it gets worse or something.

I just felt like I could see it so clearly, like I had the answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other strong beliefs or ideas that you didn’t share with others because you felt they wouldn’t understand or would think they were strange?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it’s that same craft thing, really. Not messages from another world, no. Nothing like that. It was more ordinary, I guess, but it felt huge to me at the time.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… yes, in a way. Not like I thought I was chosen by the universe or anything like that. It was more that I felt like I was the one who finally saw the whole picture.

With the crafts, I really believed I could turn it into a real retirement business right away, like this was the perfect plan for our family and everybody would see that I had figured it out. I’d be thinking about what to make, how to set it up, the little tags and the colors—because people do notice that stuff, you know, especially around holidays—and then I’d get kind of stuck on how clear it all seemed to me.

So I don’t know if that counts as a “special role,” but I did feel like I understood something the rest of them weren’t getting.

rewriting schedules, looking up genealogy records, and making lists like I couldn’t sit still.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range I’d be thinking about what to make, how to set it up, the little tags and the colors—because people do notice that stuff, you know, especially around holidays—and then I’d get kind of stuck on how clear it all seemed to me.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as a “special role,” but I did feel like I understood something the rest of them weren’t getting.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had any beliefs that you knew were not shared by others, even when there was no logical reason for you to believe them, and they were not related to your mood or feelings of confidence?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not separate from how I was feeling, I mean. That’s hard to pull apart.

Like, when I got really set on an idea, it usually came with me feeling very sure of myself, like I could see the whole thing laid out. But if you mean just some belief out of nowhere, with no reason and not tied to me feeling confident or worked up about it, I can’t really think of one.

I haven’t really been focusing on other stuff like smells or being touched or seeing things, it’s just that voice thing that scared me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that others don’t, or felt like something was visually off in your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… I don’t think that’s really been the thing. At home it’ll come from the hallway, and it sounds just like my husband is standing right there, close by, but then nobody’s there. Like not just selling a few things at a craft fair, but a real business right away, and that everyone would see I’d figured out the perfect plan for our family.\n\nI didn’t tell everybody how sure I was, because people can be negative, you know? My sister especially, she’ll say, “Well, have you priced supplies?” and then it turns into this whole thing about yarn and booths and taxes, and I’m like, no, you’re missing the point. I just felt like I could see it so clearly, like I had the answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other strong beliefs or ideas that you didn’t share with others because you felt they wouldn’t understand or would think they were strange?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it’s that same craft thing, really. I got it in my head that I wasn’t just making little things, like scarves or wreaths or whatever for fun, but that I could turn it into a real retirement business right away.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds big, but at the time it made so much sense to me. I was thinking, like, this is the plan, this is how we’re going to be okay later, and everyone was going to see that I had figured out the perfect thing for our family. It was mostly the craft business thing that got big in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that wasn’t true, like thinking someone was sending you secret messages or that you had a special connection to someone famous or powerful?\n\nPatient: No, not secret messages or famous people or anything like that. It was more… I really got stuck on this idea that I could take my crafts and turn it into a real retirement business right away.\n\nLike, I wasn’t thinking of it as just selling a few things here and there. I thought I had figured out the plan for my family, like everyone would see I was right once it got going. And then I’d start thinking about displays and colors and what my sister would say, because she always has an opinion about prices, and then I’d get irritated because it felt like people were missing the bigger picture. But no, not secret messages.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that most people would find strange or impossible, like thinking you were receiving messages from another world or that something ordinary had a special meaning just for you?\n\nPatient: Not messages from another world, no. Nothing like that. It was more ordinary, I guess, but it felt huge to me at the time.\n\nI got it in my head that my crafts could be this whole retirement business right away, like I had finally figured out the perfect plan for my family. I could see the tables and the colors and what would sell, and I’d think, why doesn’t everyone else see this? My sister, she’s always the one who brings up practical things, like prices and supplies, and normally that’s helpful, but then it just felt like she was missing what I’d figured out.\n\nSo not “special messages,” but I really believed I had the answer and everyone would see I was right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other beliefs that you knew other people didn’t share, even if you didn’t talk about them, like thinking something was meant for you specifically or that you had a special role others didn’t understand?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, in a way. Not like I thought I was chosen by the universe or anything like that. It was more that I felt like I was the one who finally saw the whole picture.\n\nWith the crafts, I really believed I could turn it into a real retirement business right away, like this was the perfect plan for our family and everybody would see that I had figured it out. I’d be thinking about what to make, how to set it up, the little tags and the colors—because people do notice that stuff, you know, especially around holidays—and then I’d get kind of stuck on how clear it all seemed to me.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as a “special role,” but I did feel like I understood something the rest of them weren’t getting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that you knew were not shared by others, even when there was no logical reason for you to believe them, and they were not related to your mood or feelings of confidence?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not separate from how I was feeling, I mean. Yeah… I mean, not voices or seeing things, no. It was more like I was just completely sure I had figured something out.

With my crafts, I really believed I could turn it into a retirement business right away. Like not someday, not “maybe if I work up to it,” but like, this is it, this is the plan. Like at home, from the hallway, and I’ll think it’s my husband because it sounds so close, like he’s right there next to me. And then I go check, and nobody’s there.\n\nAnd it happened at the warehouse once too, which is embarrassing because that place is always loud anyway, so I kept telling myself it was just the forklifts or someone calling out, but… it was my name. I haven’t really been focusing on other stuff like smells or being touched or seeing things, it’s just that voice thing that scared me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that others don’t, or felt like something was visually off in your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… I don’t think that’s really been the thing. Like, sometimes at the warehouse the lights make everything feel weird, you know, those big overhead lights and the aisles all start looking the same, and I’ll get turned around looking for a pallet label, but that’s not like seeing something that isn’t there.\n\nWhat really scared me was hearing my name called. At home it’ll come from the hallway, and it sounds just like my husband is standing right there, close by, but then nobody’s there. And it happened once at work too, at the warehouse, and I kept trying to blame it on all the noise, but it sounded real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts or beliefs that other people don’t seem to understand, or that you’ve kept to yourself because you felt others wouldn’t believe them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe. I don’t know if it counts as, like, a “belief,” exactly, but I got really convinced for a while that my crafts were going to be this whole retirement business. Like not just selling a few things at a craft fair, but a real business right away, and that everyone would see I’d figured out the perfect plan for our family.\n\nI didn’t tell everybody how sure I was, because people can be negative, you know? My sister especially, she’ll say, “Well, have you priced supplies?” and then it turns into this whole thing about yarn and booths and taxes, and I’m like, no, you’re missing the point. I just felt like I could see it so clearly, like I had the answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other strong beliefs or ideas that you didn’t share with others because you felt they wouldn’t understand or would think they were strange?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it’s that same craft thing, really. I got it in my head that I wasn’t just making little things, like scarves or wreaths or whatever for fun, but that I could turn it into a real retirement business right away.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds big, but at the time it made so much sense to me. I was thinking, like, this is the plan, this is how we’re going to be okay later, and everyone was going to see that I had figured out the perfect thing for our family. I didn’t really want to tell certain people because they’d start nitpicking it, like about supplies and prices and all that, and it felt like they just wouldn’t get what I was seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that others would say isn’t true, like feeling watched when you’re alone, or thinking you have a special ability or connection that others don’t?\n\nPatient: Not like being watched, no… I mean, I don’t think so. It was more that I really believed I had this special kind of plan, like with my crafts. I got so sure I could turn it into a real retirement business right away, not years from now or just as a hobby.\n\nAnd I know people would’ve said, “Slow down, you need numbers,” and my sister would’ve brought up booth fees and yarn prices—she does that, she’s practical—but I felt like they just weren’t seeing it. I felt like I had figured out the perfect thing for our family, and once it got going everyone would realize I was right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that you thought were true, but others would say are impossible or make no sense, even outside of the idea about your crafts?\n\nPatient: Outside of the crafts… I don’t think so? I’m trying to think. I mean, sometimes I get real sure about things, like at work I’ll think, “If they’d just let me organize the back room my way, everything would run smoother,” but that’s not impossible, that’s just common sense, honestly.\n\nBut no, not like… I don’t think I’ve had other ideas where people would say it makes no sense. It was mostly the craft business thing that got big in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that wasn’t true, like thinking someone was sending you secret messages or that you had a special connection to someone famous or powerful?\n\nPatient: No, not secret messages or famous people or anything like that. It was more… I really got stuck on this idea that I could take my crafts and turn it into a real retirement business right away.\n\nLike, I wasn’t thinking of it as just selling a few things here and there. I thought I had figured out the plan for my family, like everyone would see I was right once it got going. And then I’d start thinking about displays and colors and what my sister would say, because she always has an opinion about prices, and then I’d get irritated because it felt like people were missing the bigger picture. But no, not secret messages.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that most people would find strange or impossible, like thinking you were receiving messages from another world or that something ordinary had a special meaning just for you?\n\nPatient: Not messages from another world, no. Nothing like that. It was more ordinary, I guess, but it felt huge to me at the time.\n\nI got it in my head that my crafts could be this whole retirement business right away, like I had finally figured out the perfect plan for my family. I could see the tables and the colors and what would sell, and I’d think, why doesn’t everyone else see this? My sister, she’s always the one who brings up practical things, like prices and supplies, and normally that’s helpful, but then it just felt like she was missing what I’d figured out.\n\nSo not “special messages,” but I really believed I had the answer and everyone would see I was right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other beliefs that you knew other people didn’t share, even if you didn’t talk about them, like thinking something was meant for you specifically or that you had a special role others didn’t understand?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, in a way. Not like I thought I was chosen by the universe or anything like that. It was more that I felt like I was the one who finally saw the whole picture.\n\nWith the crafts, I really believed I could turn it into a real retirement business right away, like this was the perfect plan for our family and everybody would see that I had figured it out. I’d be thinking about what to make, how to set it up, the little tags and the colors—because people do notice that stuff, you know, especially around holidays—and then I’d get kind of stuck on how clear it all seemed to me.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as a “special role,” but I did feel like I understood something the rest of them weren’t getting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that you knew were not shared by others, even when there was no logical reason for you to believe them, and they were not related to your mood or feelings of confidence?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not separate from how I was feeling, I mean. That’s hard to pull apart.\n\nLike, when I got really set on an idea, it usually came with me feeling very sure of myself, like I could see the whole thing laid out. But if you mean just some belief out of nowhere, with no reason and not tied to me feeling confident or worked up about it, I can’t really think of one.\n\nI mean, I’ll get opinions about things—at work, like how they stock the shelves wrong or how management makes everything harder than it needs to be—but that’s not the same thing, right? That’s just… everybody has opinions. So no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of belief.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other beliefs that you were absolutely sure of, even when others couldn’t see them or prove them, and they weren’t about things like hearing voices or seeing something wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not voices or seeing things, no. It was more like I was just completely sure I had figured something out.\n\nWith my crafts, I really believed I could turn it into a retirement business right away. Like not someday, not “maybe if I work up to it,” but like, this is it, this is the plan. I could picture all of it—what I’d make, how people would buy it, how it would help my family. And I got kind of frustrated when other people couldn’t see it, because to me it felt obvious.\n\nI know now there’s supplies and pricing and all that, and my sister—she’s always been good with numbers, which is annoying sometimes but she’s usually right—but at the time I was sure everyone would eventually realize I had the perfect plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that wasn’t based in reality, and wasn’t about your crafts or how confident or emotional you were feeling at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not that I can separate out like that, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes I get really convinced about things, but usually it’s tied into whatever else is going on with me at the time, you know? Like if I’m all worked up or excited about something, then everything can feel more certain than it probably is.\n\nBut just a random belief that wasn’t real and wasn’t connected to my mood or confidence… I can’t think of one. I’d probably have to think about it more, because sometimes you don’t realize something sounded strange until later. But nothing comes to mind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had something like that, not in the way you’re saying.

I can get stubborn, though. Like, my sister would tell you I can argue over something stupid in the grocery store for twenty minutes, like whether they moved the coffee aisle for a reason or because nobody there knows how to organize anything. And at work I’ll be dead sure I know the better way to stock something, but that’s more me being irritated, I guess.

But a belief with no evidence, and not tied to me feeling up or down or extra sure of myself… I can’t think of one. If it happened, I don’t know that I recognized it that way.

I didn’t really want to tell certain people because they’d start nitpicking it, like about supplies and prices and all that, and it felt like they just wouldn’t get what I was seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that others would say isn’t true, like feeling watched when you’re alone, or thinking you have a special ability or connection that others don’t?\n\nPatient: Not like being watched, no… I mean, I don’t think so. It was more that I really believed I had this special kind of plan, like with my crafts. I got so sure I could turn it into a real retirement business right away, not years from now or just as a hobby.\n\nAnd I know people would’ve said, “Slow down, you need numbers,” and my sister would’ve brought up booth fees and yarn prices—she does that, she’s practical—but I felt like they just weren’t seeing it. It was mostly the craft business thing that got big in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that wasn’t true, like thinking someone was sending you secret messages or that you had a special connection to someone famous or powerful?\n\nPatient: No, not secret messages or famous people or anything like that. I don’t think so… not that I can pull out separately like that.

I mean, I know I’ve been completely sure about things before, and then later I’m like, okay, maybe that was a bit much. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess I’m still not coming up with anything like that. I keep trying to think of an example, but then my mind goes to regular stuff, like at work when I’m sure somebody messed with my cart or changed where things go, but that’s more because I’m already frustrated and running behind.

And then I start thinking about other things, like last week they moved the paper towels again and nobody tells anybody, so you waste half your shift walking around like an idiot—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.

So… no, I don’t think I’ve had a belief like that, where there was no proof and it wasn’t tied to how I was feeling or something specific going on. At least not one I can name.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.1, F25.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess what finally scared me enough to come in is I’ve been hearing my name called when nobody’s there. Like at home, from the hallway, and I’ll think it’s my husband, because it sounds that real, like he’s standing right next to me. But then I go look and there’s nobody.\n\nIt happened once at the warehouse too, which really threw me off, because I was stocking and it’s noisy there sometimes, forklifts and pallets and people calling out, so at first I thought, okay, maybe I just misheard. But… nobody was there. And I don’t know, I kept thinking about it all night after, and then I was like, maybe I should actually talk to somebody before it gets worse or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other types of strange sensations or perceptions, like seeing things that aren’t there, feeling something touching you when no one is, or smelling things others don’t?\n\nPatient: I mean… mostly it’s the hearing my name thing. Like at home, from the hallway, and I’ll think it’s my husband because it sounds so close, like he’s right there next to me. And then I go check, and nobody’s there.\n\nAnd it happened at the warehouse once too, which is embarrassing because that place is always loud anyway, so I kept telling myself it was just the forklifts or someone calling out, but… it was my name. I haven’t really been focusing on other stuff like smells or being touched or seeing things, it’s just that voice thing that scared me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that others don’t, or felt like something was visually off in your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… I don’t think that’s really been the thing. Like, sometimes at the warehouse the lights make everything feel weird, you know, those big overhead lights and the aisles all start looking the same, and I’ll get turned around looking for a pallet label, but that’s not like seeing something that isn’t there.\n\nWhat really scared me was hearing my name called. At home it’ll come from the hallway, and it sounds just like my husband is standing right there, close by, but then nobody’s there. And it happened once at work too, at the warehouse, and I kept trying to blame it on all the noise, but it sounded real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts or beliefs that other people don’t seem to understand, or that you’ve kept to yourself because you felt others wouldn’t believe them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe. I don’t know if it counts as, like, a “belief,” exactly, but I got really convinced for a while that my crafts were going to be this whole retirement business. Like not just selling a few things at a craft fair, but a real business right away, and that everyone would see I’d figured out the perfect plan for our family.\n\nI didn’t tell everybody how sure I was, because people can be negative, you know? My sister especially, she’ll say, “Well, have you priced supplies?” and then it turns into this whole thing about yarn and booths and taxes, and I’m like, no, you’re missing the point. I just felt like I could see it so clearly, like I had the answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other strong beliefs or ideas that you didn’t share with others because you felt they wouldn’t understand or would think they were strange?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it’s that same craft thing, really. I got it in my head that I wasn’t just making little things, like scarves or wreaths or whatever for fun, but that I could turn it into a real retirement business right away.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds big, but at the time it made so much sense to me. I was thinking, like, this is the plan, this is how we’re going to be okay later, and everyone was going to see that I had figured out the perfect thing for our family. I didn’t really want to tell certain people because they’d start nitpicking it, like about supplies and prices and all that, and it felt like they just wouldn’t get what I was seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that others would say isn’t true, like feeling watched when you’re alone, or thinking you have a special ability or connection that others don’t?\n\nPatient: Not like being watched, no… I mean, I don’t think so. It was more that I really believed I had this special kind of plan, like with my crafts. I got so sure I could turn it into a real retirement business right away, not years from now or just as a hobby.\n\nAnd I know people would’ve said, “Slow down, you need numbers,” and my sister would’ve brought up booth fees and yarn prices—she does that, she’s practical—but I felt like they just weren’t seeing it. I felt like I had figured out the perfect thing for our family, and once it got going everyone would realize I was right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that you thought were true, but others would say are impossible or make no sense, even outside of the idea about your crafts?\n\nPatient: Outside of the crafts… I don’t think so? I’m trying to think. I mean, sometimes I get real sure about things, like at work I’ll think, “If they’d just let me organize the back room my way, everything would run smoother,” but that’s not impossible, that’s just common sense, honestly.\n\nBut no, not like… I don’t think I’ve had other ideas where people would say it makes no sense. It was mostly the craft business thing that got big in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that wasn’t true, like thinking someone was sending you secret messages or that you had a special connection to someone famous or powerful?\n\nPatient: No, not secret messages or famous people or anything like that. It was more… I really got stuck on this idea that I could take my crafts and turn it into a real retirement business right away.\n\nLike, I wasn’t thinking of it as just selling a few things here and there. I thought I had figured out the plan for my family, like everyone would see I was right once it got going. And then I’d start thinking about displays and colors and what my sister would say, because she always has an opinion about prices, and then I’d get irritated because it felt like people were missing the bigger picture. But no, not secret messages.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that most people would find strange or impossible, like thinking you were receiving messages from another world or that something ordinary had a special meaning just for you?\n\nPatient: Not messages from another world, no. Nothing like that. It was more ordinary, I guess, but it felt huge to me at the time.\n\nI got it in my head that my crafts could be this whole retirement business right away, like I had finally figured out the perfect plan for my family. I could see the tables and the colors and what would sell, and I’d think, why doesn’t everyone else see this? My sister, she’s always the one who brings up practical things, like prices and supplies, and normally that’s helpful, but then it just felt like she was missing what I’d figured out.\n\nSo not “special messages,” but I really believed I had the answer and everyone would see I was right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other beliefs that you knew other people didn’t share, even if you didn’t talk about them, like thinking something was meant for you specifically or that you had a special role others didn’t understand?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, in a way. Not like I thought I was chosen by the universe or anything like that. It was more that I felt like I was the one who finally saw the whole picture.\n\nWith the crafts, I really believed I could turn it into a real retirement business right away, like this was the perfect plan for our family and everybody would see that I had figured it out. I’d be thinking about what to make, how to set it up, the little tags and the colors—because people do notice that stuff, you know, especially around holidays—and then I’d get kind of stuck on how clear it all seemed to me.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as a “special role,” but I did feel like I understood something the rest of them weren’t getting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that you knew were not shared by others, even when there was no logical reason for you to believe them, and they were not related to your mood or feelings of confidence?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not separate from how I was feeling, I mean. That’s hard to pull apart.\n\nLike, when I got really set on an idea, it usually came with me feeling very sure of myself, like I could see the whole thing laid out. But if you mean just some belief out of nowhere, with no reason and not tied to me feeling confident or worked up about it, I can’t really think of one.\n\nI mean, I’ll get opinions about things—at work, like how they stock the shelves wrong or how management makes everything harder than it needs to be—but that’s not the same thing, right? That’s just… everybody has opinions. So no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of belief.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other beliefs that you were absolutely sure of, even when others couldn’t see them or prove them, and they weren’t about things like hearing voices or seeing something wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not voices or seeing things, no. It was more like I was just completely sure I had figured something out.\n\nWith my crafts, I really believed I could turn it into a retirement business right away. Like not someday, not “maybe if I work up to it,” but like, this is it, this is the plan. I could picture all of it—what I’d make, how people would buy it, how it would help my family. And I got kind of frustrated when other people couldn’t see it, because to me it felt obvious.\n\nI know now there’s supplies and pricing and all that, and my sister—she’s always been good with numbers, which is annoying sometimes but she’s usually right—but at the time I was sure everyone would eventually realize I had the perfect plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that wasn’t based in reality, and wasn’t about your crafts or how confident or emotional you were feeling at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not that I can separate out like that, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes I get really convinced about things, but usually it’s tied into whatever else is going on with me at the time, you know? Like if I’m all worked up or excited about something, then everything can feel more certain than it probably is.\n\nBut just a random belief that wasn’t real and wasn’t connected to my mood or confidence… I can’t think of one. I’d probably have to think about it more, because sometimes you don’t realize something sounded strange until later. But nothing comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that you were absolutely certain about, even when there was no real evidence to support them and they weren’t connected to feeling particularly good, bad, or confident about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had something like that, not in the way you’re saying.\n\nI can get stubborn, though. Like, my sister would tell you I can argue over something stupid in the grocery store for twenty minutes, like whether they moved the coffee aisle for a reason or because nobody there knows how to organize anything. And at work I’ll be dead sure I know the better way to stock something, but that’s more me being irritated, I guess.\n\nBut a belief with no evidence, and not tied to me feeling up or down or extra sure of myself… I can’t think of one. If it happened, I don’t know that I recognized it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something so strongly that you were completely certain it was true, even though there was no real proof or others couldn't see it, and it wasn't about the crafts business or how you were feeling emotionally at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can pull out separately like that.\n\nI mean, I know I’ve been completely sure about things before, and then later I’m like, okay, maybe that was a bit much. But when you say not the crafts thing and not how I was feeling emotionally, then I kind of lose the thread, because most of the times I get that certain, there’s something going on around it.\n\nLike even at work, I’ll get convinced the manager changed the whole back room system just to make things harder, but then it’s probably just because I’m annoyed and the boxes are stacked wrong and everyone’s in my way. So I don’t know if that counts, but I don’t think that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I can’t think of a belief like that. Not one where there was no proof and it was just sitting there on its own.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something so strongly that you were completely certain it was true, even though there was no real proof or others couldn't see it, and it wasn't about the crafts business or how you were feeling emotionally at the time?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not coming up with anything like that. I keep trying to think of an example, but then my mind goes to regular stuff, like at work when I’m sure somebody messed with my cart or changed where things go, but that’s more because I’m already frustrated and running behind.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other things, like last week they moved the paper towels again and nobody tells anybody, so you waste half your shift walking around like an idiot—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had a belief like that, where there was no proof and it wasn’t tied to how I was feeling or something specific going on. At least not one I can name.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So I don’t know if that counts, but I don’t think that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I can’t think of a belief like that. Not one where there was no proof and it was just sitting there on its own.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something so strongly that you were completely certain it was true, even though there was no real proof or others couldn't see it, and it wasn't about the crafts business or how you were feeling emotionally at the time?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not coming up with anything like that. I keep trying to think of an example, but then my mind goes to regular stuff, like at work when I’m sure somebody messed with my cart or changed where things go, but that’s more because I’m already frustrated and running behind.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other things, like last week they moved the paper towels again and nobody tells anybody, so you waste half your shift walking around like an idiot—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had a belief like that, where there was no proof and it wasn’t tied to how I was feeling or something specific going on. At least not one I can name.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that you knew were completely true, even though there was no real evidence for them and they weren’t connected to how you were feeling at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I’m trying to think of something, but nothing really comes up that’s separate from, you know, being mad or stressed or already in a mood.\n\nLike, sometimes I’m sure people at work did something wrong, but that’s because I’m standing there looking at the shelves and the whole thing’s a mess, and then I get irritated. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I’m still kind of stuck on that one. I don’t think I’ve had that, not in the way you’re saying.

When I’m completely sure about something, there’s usually some reason I’ve latched onto, even if later it turns out I was making too much of it. Like at work, if something’s not where it’s supposed to be, I’ll be convinced someone moved it or didn’t do their job, but that’s because I’m looking at the mess right in front of me, you know? And then I get irritated and start connecting dots.

But if you mean just a belief that came out of nowhere, with no evidence, and nobody else could see it… no, I can’t really think of one. At least nothing that stands out to me.

But then I go look and there’s nobody.\n\nIt happened once at the warehouse too, which really threw me off, because I was stocking and it’s noisy there sometimes, forklifts and pallets and people calling out, so at first I thought, okay, maybe I just misheard. And I don’t know, I kept thinking about it all night after, and then I was like, maybe I should actually talk to somebody before it gets worse or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other types of strange sensations or perceptions, like seeing things that aren’t there, feeling something touching you when no one is, or smelling things others don’t?\n\nPatient: I mean… mostly it’s the hearing my name thing. Like at home, from the hallway, and I’ll think it’s my husband because it sounds so close, like he’s right there next to me. And then I go check, and nobody’s there.\n\nAnd it happened at the warehouse once too, which is embarrassing because that place is always loud anyway, so I kept telling myself it was just the forklifts or someone calling out, but… it was my name. I haven’t really been focusing on other stuff like smells or being touched or seeing things, it’s just that voice thing that scared me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that others don’t, or felt like something was visually off in your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… I don’t think that’s really been the thing. Like, sometimes at the warehouse the lights make everything feel weird, you know, those big overhead lights and the aisles all start looking the same, and I’ll get turned around looking for a pallet label, but that’s not like seeing something that isn’t there.\n\nWhat really scared me was hearing my name called. At home it’ll come from the hallway, and it sounds just like my husband is standing right there, close by, but then nobody’s there. And it happened once at work too, at the warehouse, and I kept trying to blame it on all the noise, but it sounded real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts or beliefs that other people don’t seem to understand, or that you’ve kept to yourself because you felt others wouldn’t believe them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe. I don’t know if it counts as, like, a “belief,” exactly, but I got really convinced for a while that my crafts were going to be this whole retirement business. Like not just selling a few things at a craft fair, but a real business right away, and that everyone would see I’d figured out the perfect plan for our family.\n\nI didn’t tell everybody how sure I was, because people can be negative, you know? My sister especially, she’ll say, “Well, have you priced supplies?” and then it turns into this whole thing about yarn and booths and taxes, and I’m like, no, you’re missing the point. I just felt like I could see it so clearly, like I had the answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other strong beliefs or ideas that you didn’t share with others because you felt they wouldn’t understand or would think they were strange?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it’s that same craft thing, really. I got it in my head that I wasn’t just making little things, like scarves or wreaths or whatever for fun, but that I could turn it into a real retirement business right away.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds big, but at the time it made so much sense to me. I was thinking, like, this is the plan, this is how we’re going to be okay later, and everyone was going to see that I had figured out the perfect thing for our family. I didn’t really want to tell certain people because they’d start nitpicking it, like about supplies and prices and all that, and it felt like they just wouldn’t get what I was seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that others would say isn’t true, like feeling watched when you’re alone, or thinking you have a special ability or connection that others don’t?\n\nPatient: Not like being watched, no… I mean, I don’t think so. It was more that I really believed I had this special kind of plan, like with my crafts. I got so sure I could turn it into a real retirement business right away, not years from now or just as a hobby.\n\nAnd I know people would’ve said, “Slow down, you need numbers,” and my sister would’ve brought up booth fees and yarn prices—she does that, she’s practical—but I felt like they just weren’t seeing it. I felt like I had figured out the perfect thing for our family, and once it got going everyone would realize I was right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that you thought were true, but others would say are impossible or make no sense, even outside of the idea about your crafts?\n\nPatient: Outside of the crafts… I don’t think so? I’m trying to think. I mean, sometimes I get real sure about things, like at work I’ll think, “If they’d just let me organize the back room my way, everything would run smoother,” but that’s not impossible, that’s just common sense, honestly.\n\nBut no, not like… I don’t think I’ve had other ideas where people would say it makes no sense. It was mostly the craft business thing that got big in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that wasn’t true, like thinking someone was sending you secret messages or that you had a special connection to someone famous or powerful?\n\nPatient: No, not secret messages or famous people or anything like that. It was more… I really got stuck on this idea that I could take my crafts and turn it into a real retirement business right away.\n\nLike, I wasn’t thinking of it as just selling a few things here and there. I thought I had figured out the plan for my family, like everyone would see I was right once it got going. And then I’d start thinking about displays and colors and what my sister would say, because she always has an opinion about prices, and then I’d get irritated because it felt like people were missing the bigger picture. But no, not secret messages.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that most people would find strange or impossible, like thinking you were receiving messages from another world or that something ordinary had a special meaning just for you?\n\nPatient: Not messages from another world, no. Nothing like that. It was more ordinary, I guess, but it felt huge to me at the time.\n\nI got it in my head that my crafts could be this whole retirement business right away, like I had finally figured out the perfect plan for my family. I could see the tables and the colors and what would sell, and I’d think, why doesn’t everyone else see this? My sister, she’s always the one who brings up practical things, like prices and supplies, and normally that’s helpful, but then it just felt like she was missing what I’d figured out.\n\nSo not “special messages,” but I really believed I had the answer and everyone would see I was right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other beliefs that you knew other people didn’t share, even if you didn’t talk about them, like thinking something was meant for you specifically or that you had a special role others didn’t understand?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, in a way. Not like I thought I was chosen by the universe or anything like that. It was more that I felt like I was the one who finally saw the whole picture.\n\nWith the crafts, I really believed I could turn it into a real retirement business right away, like this was the perfect plan for our family and everybody would see that I had figured it out. I’d be thinking about what to make, how to set it up, the little tags and the colors—because people do notice that stuff, you know, especially around holidays—and then I’d get kind of stuck on how clear it all seemed to me.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as a “special role,” but I did feel like I understood something the rest of them weren’t getting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that you knew were not shared by others, even when there was no logical reason for you to believe them, and they were not related to your mood or feelings of confidence?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not separate from how I was feeling, I mean. That’s hard to pull apart.\n\nLike, when I got really set on an idea, it usually came with me feeling very sure of myself, like I could see the whole thing laid out. But if you mean just some belief out of nowhere, with no reason and not tied to me feeling confident or worked up about it, I can’t really think of one.\n\nI mean, I’ll get opinions about things—at work, like how they stock the shelves wrong or how management makes everything harder than it needs to be—but that’s not the same thing, right? That’s just… everybody has opinions. So no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of belief.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other beliefs that you were absolutely sure of, even when others couldn’t see them or prove them, and they weren’t about things like hearing voices or seeing something wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not voices or seeing things, no. It was more like I was just completely sure I had figured something out.\n\nWith my crafts, I really believed I could turn it into a retirement business right away. Like not someday, not “maybe if I work up to it,” but like, this is it, this is the plan. I could picture all of it—what I’d make, how people would buy it, how it would help my family. And I got kind of frustrated when other people couldn’t see it, because to me it felt obvious.\n\nI know now there’s supplies and pricing and all that, and my sister—she’s always been good with numbers, which is annoying sometimes but she’s usually right—but at the time I was sure everyone would eventually realize I had the perfect plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that wasn’t based in reality, and wasn’t about your crafts or how confident or emotional you were feeling at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not that I can separate out like that, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes I get really convinced about things, but usually it’s tied into whatever else is going on with me at the time, you know? I’d be thinking about what to make, how to set it up, the little tags and the colors—because people do notice that stuff, you know, especially around holidays—and then I’d get kind of stuck on how clear it all seemed to me.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as a “special role,” but I did feel like I understood something the rest of them weren’t getting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that you knew were not shared by others, even when there was no logical reason for you to believe them, and they were not related to your mood or feelings of confidence?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not separate from how I was feeling, I mean. That’s hard to pull apart.\n\nLike, when I got really set on an idea, it usually came with me feeling very sure of myself, like I could see the whole thing laid out. But if you mean just some belief out of nowhere, with no reason and not tied to me feeling confident or worked up about it, I can’t really think of one.\n\nI mean, I’ll get opinions about things—at work, like how they stock the shelves wrong or how management makes everything harder than it needs to be—but that’s not the same thing, right? That’s just… everybody has opinions. So no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of belief.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other beliefs that you were absolutely sure of, even when others couldn’t see them or prove them, and they weren’t about things like hearing voices or seeing something wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not voices or seeing things, no. It was more like I was just completely sure I had figured something out.\n\nWith my crafts, I really believed I could turn it into a retirement business right away. Like not someday, not “maybe if I work up to it,” but like, this is it, this is the plan. I could picture all of it—what I’d make, how people would buy it, how it would help my family. And I got kind of frustrated when other people couldn’t see it, because to me it felt obvious.\n\nI know now there’s supplies and pricing and all that, and my sister—she’s always been good with numbers, which is annoying sometimes but she’s usually right—but at the time I was sure everyone would eventually realize I had the perfect plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that wasn’t based in reality, and wasn’t about your crafts or how confident or emotional you were feeling at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not that I can separate out like that, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes I get really convinced about things, but usually it’s tied into whatever else is going on with me at the time, you know? Like if I’m all worked up or excited about something, then everything can feel more certain than it probably is.\n\nBut just a random belief that wasn’t real and wasn’t connected to my mood or confidence… I can’t think of one. I’d probably have to think about it more, because sometimes you don’t realize something sounded strange until later. But nothing comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that you were absolutely certain about, even when there was no real evidence to support them and they weren’t connected to feeling particularly good, bad, or confident about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had something like that, not in the way you’re saying.\n\nI can get stubborn, though. Like, my sister would tell you I can argue over something stupid in the grocery store for twenty minutes, like whether they moved the coffee aisle for a reason or because nobody there knows how to organize anything. And at work I’ll be dead sure I know the better way to stock something, but that’s more me being irritated, I guess.\n\nBut a belief with no evidence, and not tied to me feeling up or down or extra sure of myself… I can’t think of one. If it happened, I don’t know that I recognized it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something so strongly that you were completely certain it was true, even though there was no real proof or others couldn't see it, and it wasn't about the crafts business or how you were feeling emotionally at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can pull out separately like that.\n\nI mean, I know I’ve been completely sure about things before, and then later I’m like, okay, maybe that was a bit much. But when you say not the crafts thing and not how I was feeling emotionally, then I kind of lose the thread, because most of the times I get that certain, there’s something going on around it.\n\nLike even at work, I’ll get convinced the manager changed the whole back room system just to make things harder, but then it’s probably just because I’m annoyed and the boxes are stacked wrong and everyone’s in my way. So I don’t know if that counts, but I don’t think that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I can’t think of a belief like that. Not one where there was no proof and it was just sitting there on its own.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something so strongly that you were completely certain it was true, even though there was no real proof or others couldn't see it, and it wasn't about the crafts business or how you were feeling emotionally at the time?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not coming up with anything like that. At least not one I can name.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that you knew were completely true, even though there was no real evidence for them and they weren’t connected to how you were feeling at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I’m trying to think of something, but nothing really comes up that’s separate from, you know, being mad or stressed or already in a mood.\n\nLike, sometimes I’m sure people at work did something wrong, but that’s because I’m standing there looking at the shelves and the whole thing’s a mess, and then I get irritated. My sister especially, she’ll say, “Well, have you priced supplies?” and then it turns into this whole thing about yarn and booths and taxes, and I’m like, no, you’re missing the point. I felt like I had figured out the perfect thing for our family, and once it got going everyone would realize I was right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that you thought were true, but others would say are impossible or make no sense, even outside of the idea about your crafts?\n\nPatient: Outside of the crafts… I don’t think so? I’m trying to think. I mean, sometimes I get real sure about things, like at work I’ll think, “If they’d just let me organize the back room my way, everything would run smoother,” but that’s not impossible, that’s just common sense, honestly.\n\nBut no, not like… I don’t think I’ve had other ideas where people would say it makes no sense. It was mostly the craft business thing that got big in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that wasn’t true, like thinking someone was sending you secret messages or that you had a special connection to someone famous or powerful?\n\nPatient: No, not secret messages or famous people or anything like that. It was more… I really got stuck on this idea that I could take my crafts and turn it into a real retirement business right away.\n\nLike, I wasn’t thinking of it as just selling a few things here and there. I thought I had figured out the plan for my family, like everyone would see I was right once it got going. And then I’d start thinking about displays and colors and what my sister would say, because she always has an opinion about prices, and then I’d get irritated because it felt like people were missing the bigger picture. But no, not secret messages.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that most people would find strange or impossible, like thinking you were receiving messages from another world or that something ordinary had a special meaning just for you?\n\nPatient: Not messages from another world, no. Nothing like that. It was more ordinary, I guess, but it felt huge to me at the time.\n\nI got it in my head that my crafts could be this whole retirement business right away, like I had finally figured out the perfect plan for my family. I could see the tables and the colors and what would sell, and I’d think, why doesn’t everyone else see this? My sister, she’s always the one who brings up practical things, like prices and supplies, and normally that’s helpful, but then it just felt like she was missing what I’d figured out.\n\nSo not “special messages,” but I really believed I had the answer and everyone would see I was right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other beliefs that you knew other people didn’t share, even if you didn’t talk about them, like thinking something was meant for you specifically or that you had a special role others didn’t understand?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, in a way. Not like I thought I was chosen by the universe or anything like that. It was more that I felt like I was the one who finally saw the whole picture.\n\nWith the crafts, I really believed I could turn it into a real retirement business right away, like this was the perfect plan for our family and everybody would see that I had figured it out. I’d be thinking about what to make, how to set it up, the little tags and the colors—because people do notice that stuff, you know, especially around holidays—and then I’d get kind of stuck on how clear it all seemed to me.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as a “special role,” but I did feel like I understood something the rest of them weren’t getting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that you knew were not shared by others, even when there was no logical reason for you to believe them, and they were not related to your mood or feelings of confidence?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not separate from how I was feeling, I mean. That’s hard to pull apart.\n\nLike, when I got really set on an idea, it usually came with me feeling very sure of myself, like I could see the whole thing laid out. But if you mean just some belief out of nowhere, with no reason and not tied to me feeling confident or worked up about it, I can’t really think of one.\n\nI mean, I’ll get opinions about things—at work, like how they stock the shelves wrong or how management makes everything harder than it needs to be—but that’s not the same thing, right? That’s just… everybody has opinions. So no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of belief.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other beliefs that you were absolutely sure of, even when others couldn’t see them or prove them, and they weren’t about things like hearing voices or seeing something wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not voices or seeing things, no. It was more like I was just completely sure I had figured something out.\n\nWith my crafts, I really believed I could turn it into a retirement business right away. Like not someday, not “maybe if I work up to it,” but like, this is it, this is the plan. I could picture all of it—what I’d make, how people would buy it, how it would help my family. And I got kind of frustrated when other people couldn’t see it, because to me it felt obvious.\n\nI know now there’s supplies and pricing and all that, and my sister—she’s always been good with numbers, which is annoying sometimes but she’s usually right—but at the time I was sure everyone would eventually realize I had the perfect plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that wasn’t based in reality, and wasn’t about your crafts or how confident or emotional you were feeling at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not that I can separate out like that, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes I get really convinced about things, but usually it’s tied into whatever else is going on with me at the time, you know? Like if I’m all worked up or excited about something, then everything can feel more certain than it probably is.\n\nBut just a random belief that wasn’t real and wasn’t connected to my mood or confidence… I can’t think of one. I’d probably have to think about it more, because sometimes you don’t realize something sounded strange until later. But nothing comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or ideas that you were absolutely certain about, even when there was no real evidence to support them and they weren’t connected to feeling particularly good, bad, or confident about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had something like that, not in the way you’re saying.\n\nI can get stubborn, though. Like, my sister would tell you I can argue over something stupid in the grocery store for twenty minutes, like whether they moved the coffee aisle for a reason or because nobody there knows how to organize anything. And at work I’ll be dead sure I know the better way to stock something, but that’s more me being irritated, I guess.\n\nBut a belief with no evidence, and not tied to me feeling up or down or extra sure of myself… I can’t think of one. If it happened, I don’t know that I recognized it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something so strongly that you were completely certain it was true, even though there was no real proof or others couldn't see it, and it wasn't about the crafts business or how you were feeling emotionally at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can pull out separately like that.\n\nI mean, I know I’ve been completely sure about things before, and then later I’m like, okay, maybe that was a bit much. But when you say not the crafts thing and not how I was feeling emotionally, then I kind of lose the thread, because most of the times I get that certain, there’s something going on around it.\n\nLike even at work, I’ll get convinced the manager changed the whole back room system just to make things harder, but then it’s probably just because I’m annoyed and the boxes are stacked wrong and everyone’s in my way. So I don’t know if that counts, but I don’t think that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I can’t think of a belief like that. Not one where there was no proof and it was just sitting there on its own.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something so strongly that you were completely certain it was true, even though there was no real proof or others couldn't see it, and it wasn't about the crafts business or how you were feeling emotionally at the time?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not coming up with anything like that. I keep trying to think of an example, but then my mind goes to regular stuff, like at work when I’m sure somebody messed with my cart or changed where things go, but that’s more because I’m already frustrated and running behind.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other things, like last week they moved the paper towels again and nobody tells anybody, so you waste half your shift walking around like an idiot—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had a belief like that, where there was no proof and it wasn’t tied to how I was feeling or something specific going on. At least not one I can name.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that you knew were completely true, even though there was no real evidence for them and they weren’t connected to how you were feeling at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so.
